# Nature of Politics and Communitarianism — Mains-Focused Live Edition

A fast-preparation edition built for answer-writing. It teaches what raises the mark on a question about what politics *is*, what makes a situation political, what makes a decision bind, and the three rival images of society that generate three rival accounts of politics — and it stops there. The comprehensive twenty-four-lesson session for this topic remains the unchanged reference layer; this is neither a copy nor a condensation of it, and it is not a substitute for it when depth is actually wanted.

**The one idea that organises everything.** The source chapter is not a survey of three ideologies. It is one question asked twice. First: *what kind of activity is politics at all?* — answered by an escalating sequence of definitions that ends in a test for a political situation and an apparatus for authoritative allocation. Then, having established that politics arises from conflicting demands, the chapter asks its own second question in its own words: **"what is the nature of that conflict and what is the outcome of this process? In order to answer this question, different positions can be taken"** (PDF p.100). The liberal, Marxist and communitarian views are **answers to that question**, not three creeds placed side by side. And the chapter closes not by choosing among them but by indexing each to a social condition: **"None of these views of politics may be treated as universally acceptable"** (p.106). Place a stem inside that structure and it becomes answerable. Treat the topic as three ideologies to be recited and the answer collapses into a list, which is exactly what every *critically examine*, *how far* and *do you agree* directive punishes hardest.

**Scope.** This is **Topic 07 of the subject's twenty-three topics**, and it remains a standalone session; every other topic keeps its own file. Topic 07 is **not** merged into Topic 06, Topic 08 or Topic 21, and it is **not** merged into any Philosophy `socio-political` owner. ❗ **Political Theory owns zero General Studies routes — zero GS-I, zero GS-II, zero GS-III, zero GS-IV and zero Essay.** The Commission prints no Political Theory paper. ❗ Further, **zero clauses of `Political-Theory\OFFICIAL-UPSC-SYLLABUS-MAPPING.md` name this topic at all**: a machine count of every `basic/NN_` reference in that file returns 03 x4, 04 x2, 05 x6, 10 x2, 15 x2, 17 x2, 18 x2, 19 x4, 22 x10 and 23 x4 — **07 does not appear**, not even under the Essay clause, which names `00_Master-Framework.md`, 19, 22 and 23. That is a finding, not a gap to be repaired. ❗ And a `Political-Theory` reference count across **all eleven** General Studies, Essay and Ethics routing ledgers for 2018–2026 returns **zero in every one of them**. No alias, proxy, mirror ledger or synthetic route is created here to give this subject a non-zero route count, and no previous-year question is re-routed by this pass. Political Theory is **supplementary conceptual support**, never a named question owner.

❗❗ **The finding that shapes this edition more than any other, and it is unusual enough to state on the first screen. This topic has ZERO uncontested stems.** Across the verified 126-part Philosophy Optional corpus for 2018–2026, **not one stem names this topic's subject matter**: a full-text search of both ledgers for *communitarian*, *MacIntyre*, *Sandel*, *Taylor* and *nature of politics* returns **zero matches in nine years**. Fourteen stems have a genuine Topic 07 **contribution** — and **every one of the fourteen is already claimed by a released, indexed sibling Mains edition**, most of them from a richer chapter. **Three are Decisive in Topic 21** (2025 Q4(c), 2023 Q2(b), 2018 Q1(a)); **one is Decisive in Topic 23** (2026 Q1(d), which has an entire lesson built on it); the rest sit with Topics 01, 16, 17 and 20. ⚠️ The consequence is a rule, and it governs every lesson below: **this edition is built on what the source alone owns, not on demand.** Each lesson names the stems its apparatus serves, names the **actual owner**, and names the **one move** Topic 07 adds. ❌ No lesson presents a stem as this topic's to answer, and ❌ no sibling's Decisive claim is duplicated.

**Source span — already correct, and deliberately not "corrected".** The topic runs **PDF pp.94–107 (printed pp.80–93, local offset +14)** of O. P. Gauba, *An Introduction to Political Theory*, Fifth Edition (2009), **Ch.3 `Nature of Politics`**. ✅ Both canonical owners declare **pp.94-107**, and that is right at both ends, confirmed by three independent routes: the Contents page at PDF p.9 prints `3. Nature of Politics ... 80` and `4. Approaches to the Study of Politics ... 94`; folio arithmetic gives PDF p.95 = folio 81 and PDF p.107 = folio 93, an offset of **+14** at both ends; and the physical boundaries hold, with PDF p.93 closing Ch.2 on the feminist-justice formula (Topic 06) and PDF p.108 opening Ch.4 with a 40 pt numeral `4` and a 26 pt title (Topic 08). ❌ No correction is manufactured. ⚠️ The offset is **not** constant across the volume: this chapter and Topic 06's pp.84–93 range run at **+14**, but the Gramsci range runs at **+19** and the Ch.4 Marxian-analysis range at **+15**. ❌ Never extrapolate one offset to another chapter.

❗ **The finding that makes the book, not the owners, the operative source here.** A token-presence test of **204** source-printed items against the concatenated text of **both** canonical owners returned **156 ABSENT — 76.5 per cent.** The owners are an accurate *argument skeleton* of Gauba Ch.3 and a very poor *inventory* of it. What is missing is not decoration. Every definitional authority except Easton — **Alan Ball, Stephen L. Wasby, J.D.B. Miller, Geoffrey K. Roberts, Michael Curtis** — is named in neither owner, with none of their titles or dates. Easton's own book title and date, his *web of decisions*, his *selection among alternatives* and ❗ his **unavoidability claim** are all absent. The whole of the printed section `GENERAL CHARACTERISTICS OF POLITICS` is absent: *politicization* does not appear in either owner in any form, and none of the five characteristic sub-heads is reproduced. Both Marxist block quotations — Engels 1884 and the *Communist Manifesto* 1848 — are absent with their titles, dates and wording, as are *illusion of consent*, *ideological doses*, *reverse gear* and *wither away*. Miliband's block quotation, its title and its date are absent. Locke's, Marx's, Engels's, Rousseau's and Green's dates are absent, and **Hegel's name entirely**. **Six of the eight rows** of the printed p.107 chart are absent, and **all three definition boxes**. ⚠️ Worse for this lane: the forty-eight items that *are* present cluster almost entirely in the chart's three *Basic Principle* cells and the three communitarian thinkers of p.105 — which is precisely the material **Topic 21 already teaches from a richer chapter**. The owners therefore preserve what this topic must *route away* and discard what it must *own*. For a Mains lane, where the mark is earned by named evidence attached to a claim, that makes the **OCR layer the operative source** for the decisive and useful inventories, with the owners supplying the spine, the trap bank, the two objection–reply chains, the precursor caution, the factual-risk discipline and the answer architecture.

**What is deliberately left out, and why.** The eleven author–title citations of p.94 (Sidgwick, Laski, Lipset, Catlin, Lucas and their five titles) are removed as **bibliographic accumulation** under the Generation Rules, and this is the **only** place in this edition where removal is authorised on that ground — D4's four institutional examples are kept, because they are *evidence* for a stated claim rather than a reading list. Also removed: the full provenance litigation of the extension names; bibliographic apparatus beyond titles and years actually deployed; the per-lesson repetition of the dating caution, stated once here and once at Lesson 10; the narrative reconstruction of the OCR restoration, compressed to a single disclosure at Lesson 11 and the Source Ledger; the minor illustration catalogues reproduced twice; and encyclopaedic register notes. ❌ Nothing is removed from the decisive inventory, the useful inventory, the standing qualifications, the bounded enrichment corpus, the current-affairs policy, the boundary routing or the risk register. Definitions, causal logic, the chapter's own objections and concessions, deployment, answer frameworks and required practice are kept intact.

## Roadmap

Eleven lessons. The count is **derived from the chapter's own printed architecture and from the overlap position above**, not imposed. The Generation Rules permit eight to twelve; the comprehensive session uses twenty-four.

**Start: ten printed blocks**, confirmed by font and size rather than by eye — the unheaded opening (pp.94–95); `THE POLITICAL SITUATION`; `POLITICS AS 'AUTHORITATIVE ALLOCATION OF VALUES'`; `GENERAL CHARACTERISTICS OF POLITICS` with its five sub-heads; the unheaded pp.100–101 block; `THE LIBERAL VIEW`; `THE MARXIST VIEW`; `THE COMMUNITARIAN VIEW`; `CONCLUSION`; and the p.107 chart with its three attached definition boxes.

| 3 | Easton and Roberts: what makes a decision bind, and why politics cannot be abolished | 96–97 | The unavoidability claim is decisive for 2021 Q3(a) and is held by no sibling; Easton's three terms are the theory the owners reduce to a slogan | All three |

⚠️ **How the count was fixed.** **One split is forced by the printed architecture.** `GENERAL CHARACTERISTICS OF POLITICS` carries *politicization* plus five characteristics across pp.97–99 — the densest printed stretch in the chapter and the one wholly absent from both owners. It cannot hold one concept check and one practice question. It splits into **politicization + C1 + C2** (Lesson 4) and **C3 + C4 + C5 with the fork** (Lesson 5), and the split sits exactly where the chapter's own typography changes: C1 and C2 are set in Serif-BoldItal 12 pt, C3 to C5 in Sans-Italic 11 pt. **Two compressions are adopted.** The opening block loses its bibliography, as described above. And the three communitarian thinkers stay at the depth p.105 prints, with the differentiated mechanisms — practices and tradition, recognition, the unencumbered self — **routed**, because Topic 21's released Lesson 6 already teaches the four communitarians and the machinery problem from Ch.21. **Two fusions were tested and rejected.** Merging `CONCLUSION` into the communitarian lesson was rejected: the Conclusion is not a summary but contains Miliband's quotation, the compromise/cooperation test, the functional/dysfunctional antithesis, the historical verdict and the three scope-conditions, four of which are absent from both owners and three of which are separately examinable. Merging the p.107 chart into the Conclusion was rejected: the chart is the topic's answer engine, it carries two rows the advanced owner's matrix omits, and its Exponents row carries two live uncertainties that must be flagged beside it. **One addition is required by the contract**: a final answer-architecture lesson, because this topic's answer-writing problem is unusual — it must teach the candidate to **contribute without claiming**, a skill no other lane in this subject needs as sharply. **10 blocks + 1 split + 1 architecture lesson, with 2 compressions absorbed rather than removed = 11.**

**No lesson is allocated to** — and each of the following appears only as a pointer with its actual owner named: the theory of rights, duties, obligation and accountability, the individual–state relation, Plato's and Aristotle's political theory and social contract theory (`Philosophy\paper-2\socio-political\Individual-and-State.md`); anarchism, Marxism and socialism as **ideologies** and the Marxist critique of pure democracy as a syllabus item (`…\Political-Ideologies.md`); multiculturalism, group-differentiated rights, recognition, humanism and secularism (`…\Humanism-Secularism-Multiculturalism.md`); liberal democracy as a **form of government** (`…\Forms-of-Government.md`); justice, liberty, equality and the ideals triad, and Rawls, Nozick and Sen (`…\Social-Political-Ideals.md`); development and social progress (`…\Development-Social-Progress.md`); **caste** in every form, since the chapter's entire treatment of it is the parenthesis *(e.g. politicization of caste)* (`…\Caste-Gandhi-Ambedkar.md` and the Indian-Society caste owners); reserved seats, reservation policy, Fundamental Rights, Directive Principles, Fundamental Duties, tax law and any article or statute (`Polity\…` and `Social-Justice\…` owners); and interest groups and civil society as **institutions** (`Governance\…` and `Polity\…` owners). Within this subject: the public/private demarcation from Ch.1 is **Topic 01**'s; the concept of ideology is **Topic 02**'s; liberalism's tenets are **Topic 03**'s; historical and dialectical materialism are **Topic 04**'s; anarchism and Gandhism are **Topic 05**'s; gender substance is **Topic 06**'s; systems analysis as a **method** is **Topic 08**'s; interdisciplinarity and political culture as a developed concept are **Topic 09**'s; theories of the state are **Topics 10 and 13**'s; political obligation is **Topic 14**'s; power, authority and legitimacy as doctrine are **Topic 15**'s; citizenship is **Topic 16**'s; rights doctrine is **Topic 17**'s; liberty, equality and property as doctrines are **Topic 18**'s; justice is **Topics 19 and 20**'s; ❗ the **content** of the common good, Walzer, Green's 1882 *Lectures*, the machinery problem and the printed critical appraisal of communitarianism are **Topic 21**'s; democracy as a form of government is **Topic 22**'s; and the theory of revolution and the Marxist critique of liberal democracy as a sham are **Topic 23**'s.

## Five standing qualifications

Each is stated **once, here**, and assumed thereafter. ❌ They are not repeated lesson by lesson.

- **Q1 — This topic owns no question, and its best fits are already owned by siblings.** Not a GS stem, not an Essay prompt, not a syllabus clause, and not one of the 126 Optional parts. Of the fourteen stems it genuinely serves, **three are Decisive in Topic 21 and one is Decisive in Topic 23**. ❌ Every listing in this file is a **contribution** claim; ❌ no synthetic route is built; ❌ no sibling's Decisive claim is duplicated. ⚠️ Demand is used here to **weight depth, not to claim routes**.
- **Q2 — The chapter contradicts itself twice, and both contradictions are assets.** *Authority does not signify the use of brute force* (p.97) stands against *purely from fear of consequences* (p.99); and *an authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable* (p.97) stands against *the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself* (p.104). The chapter stages neither collision. ❌ Do not smooth them. ❗ Teach them as the openings a critical answer is looking for — they are staged deliberately at Lessons 5, 8 and 10, and marked as this session's staging rather than the chapter's.
- **Q3 — Every illustration in this chapter is 2009 or earlier, and most are unsourced.** The reserved-seat and tax examples, MacIntyre's symptom list, the USSR and China verdicts and every regime illustration under politicization are Fifth-Edition statements with no study, no country, no rate, no statute and no date attached. They travel **as the chapter's claims, with their status attached**, or not at all. ⚠️ The chapter's only dated events are **1917** and **1949**; its only dated texts are 1848, 1884, 1953, 1962, 1971, 1972, 1977, 1978, 1981, 1982, 1985 and 1988.
- **Q4 — Hedges are load-bearing on this topic more than on most.** *not very deep*; *no **basic** conflict*; a ***well-functioning** community*; *at least in relation to class societies*; ***extremely** difficult to realize*; *with **little effect** on public life*; *usually*; *sometimes*; *may ultimately*; *by no means universal*. ❌ Dropping any one of them converts a defensible claim into a caricature, and each is the clause an examiner uses to separate a read candidate from a remembered one.
- **Q5 — Communitarianism here is a theory of POLITICS, not a theory of the good.** The chapter's own clause is the licence: communitarianism **"redefines politics as the pursuit of the common good rather than as an attempt at resolution of some pre-existing conflict"** (p.100). ❌ The content of the common good, its distinction from common interest and public interest, Green's conditional obligation, Michael Walzer and the chapter-level critical appraisal of communitarianism are **Topic 21's**, and the boundary is stated explicitly and at length in Lesson 9.

A stem asks *what is the nature of politics?* and the candidate begins: "Politics is the study of the state and government." The answer is already two marks down, and the chapter's first page explains exactly why. The word is **ambiguous** — it names an activity and also names the study of that activity — and before anything can be said about the nature of politics, the writer has to say which one is under discussion and then fix the object. The chapter does this in four sentences, and then spends a page clearing away the **two** specific misconceptions that stop most people from seeing the activity at all. ⚠️ Neither owner states the ambiguity, names either misconception, or reproduces the thesis sentence that closes the block. This lesson is therefore wholly the book's, and it is the opening paragraph for every stem this topic serves.

```text
            |                              |      Politics and Economics
            |                              |    LONDON SCHOOL of Economics
            |                              |      and Political Science
            |                              |
            |                              '> 'OFTEN USED SYNONYMOUSLY
            |                                  WITH POLITICAL SCIENCE'
            |
            '====> WHICHEVER SENSE IS USED, THE OBJECT IS THE SAME:
                   *** 'OUR SUBJECT OF STUDY IS ALWAYS CONCERNED WITH A
                        PARTICULAR TYPE OF HUMAN ACTIVITY' ***
                   >> so the question is: WHAT IS THE NATURE OF THAT
                      ACTIVITY?

  ///////////////////  DISCARD BAND  ///////////////////////////////////
  //  BALL'S PROBLEM [1] -- INSTITUTIONAL NARROWING                   //
  //  'ONLY CONCERNED WITH THE PUBLIC SECTOR, WITH PARLIAMENTS,       //
  //   ELECTIONS, CABINETS, AND HAS LITTLE RELEVANCE TO OTHER         //
  //   SPHERES OF HUMAN ACTIVITY'                                     //
  //                                                                  //
  //  BALL'S PROBLEM [2] -- PARTISAN REDUCTION                        //
  //  'CONFUSING POLITICS SOLELY WITH PARTY POLITICS ... A DISTASTE   //
  //   WITH THE INTRIGUES AND TRICKS OF PARTY POLITICIANS SEEKING     //
  //   POWER'                                                         //
  //                                                                  //
  //  THE LAYMAN'S CATALOGUE (struck through -- NOT the definition)   //
  //  ~public meetings~ ~processions~ ~slogans~ ~demonstrations~      //
  //  ~demands~ ~strikes~ ~tear-gas~ ~lathi-charge~ ~elections with   //
  //  false promises and false reports on their fulfilment~           //
  //    => 'THE LAST RESORT OF SCOUNDRELS'  |  'A DIRTY GAME'         //
  //    => students advised to keep away; judges and intellectuals    //
  //       'EXPECTED TO KEEP THEMSELVES ABOVE POLITICS'               //
  /////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////////
  CAPTION: Ernest Benn -- politics is 'THE ART OF LOOKING FOR TROUBLE,
  FINDING IT WHERE IT EXISTS OR NOT, DIAGNOSING IT WRONGLY, AND
  APPLYING THE WRONG REMEDY'.  The band is DISCARDED, but it is
  discarded WITH A REASON: 'SUCH VAGUE ASSUMPTIONS ARE THE REASON WHY
  POLITICS OFTEN GETS DUBBED ...' -- the insults are an EXPLANANDUM.

            ||
            \/
  THE REPLACEMENT, IN TWO STEPS (p.95)
  [OLD] 'polis' -> citizens 'IN THEIR ROLE AS MEMBERS AND OPERATORS OF
        THE STATE' -> 'POLITICAL SCIENCE' AS THE 'SCIENCE OF THE STATE'
        (a) a study of THE INSTITUTIONS OF THE STATE
        (b) developing ideas concerning THE NATURE OF A PERFECT STATE
  [NEW] politics 'DOES NOT OPERATE STRICTLY WITHIN THE FRAMEWORK OF AN
        INSTITUTIONAL SET-UP BUT PERMEATES THE ENTIRE SOCIAL FABRIC'
        *** 'POLITICS IS NOW TREATED AS A SOCIAL PROCESS RATHER THAN AN
             AGGREGATE OF THE FORMAL INSTITUTIONS OF THE STATE' ***
```

*One word, two senses, one object; then a band of popular images discarded with a stated reason; then a two-limbed traditional programme replaced by a one-sentence thesis. The whole of Lesson 1 is visible in the diagram, and the starred lines are the two sentences an opening paragraph is built from.*

### 1. The ambiguity, and why naming it buys a mark

The chapter opens: **"The term 'politics' is applied to a particular social phenomenon as well as to a systematic study of that phenomenon."** It then gives the historical instance — **"When Aristotle adopted *Politics* as the title of his famous work, he used the term to indicate a distinct branch of study"** — and the usage finding: **"the term 'politics' is often used synonymously with 'political science'."**

⚠️ Neither owner states this at all. It matters for two reasons. First, it is a **small act of precision that an examiner notices in the first line**: the candidate who writes *politics in the sense of an activity, not in the sense of the discipline* has signalled, before any content, that the question has been read. Second, it is what makes the chapter's next move intelligible. If *politics* were only a discipline, asking after its nature would be asking after a syllabus. The chapter forecloses that reading explicitly: ⭐ **"Whether we use the term 'politics' or 'political science', our subject of study is always concerned with a particular type of human activity."** Six words of that sentence — *always concerned with human activity* — fix the object for the rest of the chapter, and for the rest of this edition.

### 2. The institutional evidence, which is what converts an assertion into evidence

The chapter does not merely assert that usage is interchangeable; it **proves it with named institutions**: the **University of Bombay** has a *Department of Civics and Politics* while the **University of Delhi** maintains a *Department of Political Science*, and the same variation appears between the **Gokhale Institute of Politics and Economics** and the **London School of Economics and Political Science**.

❗ Absent from both owners, and disproportionately useful. Two of the four are **Indian**, which means the claim can be evidenced in an Indian-context stem without importing a single unverified fact. ❌ Nothing further about any of the four institutions may be asserted from this topic — no founding date, no faculty, no curriculum, no comparison of their teaching. The chapter uses them as **nomenclature evidence** and nothing else, and so does this edition. ⚠️ Note also that this is the **only** part of the p.94 bibliography retained: the eleven author–title citations surrounding it are bibliographic accumulation and are removed, whereas these four are evidence for a stated claim.

### 3. Ball's two problems — two misconceptions, not one

Alan Ball, in ***Modern Politics and Government* (1988)**, is credited with indicating **two** problems arising from general impressions about political activity. ❗ They are separable, and the separation is the mark.

**Problem [1], institutional narrowing.** **"It is often assumed that politics is only concerned with the public sector, with parliaments, elections, cabinets, and has little relevance to other spheres of human activity."**

**Problem [2], partisan reduction.** **"There is the danger of confusing politics solely with party politics, that it is somehow concerned with having a political opinion, or that it at least implies a distaste with the intrigues and tricks of party politicians seeking power."**

⚠️ The basic owner's trap list offers a single compressed version — *politics means only party competition or elections* — which fuses the two. They are not the same error and they do not have the same cure. [1] is an error about **scope**: it confines politics to a sector, and it is corrected by the process turn in §5 below, which says politics *permeates the entire social fabric*. [2] is an error about **content**: it confuses the activity with the conduct of a particular class of its practitioners, and it is corrected by fixing the object as a type of activity rather than as a profession. ⭐ **An opening paragraph that names both, in order, and attaches the right correction to each, is a ready-made first forty words for any stem on the nature of politics.** 📝 The scan prints `contusing` for *confusing* in [2]; the intended word is reproduced here and the raw form is disclosed in the Source Ledger as normalisation N2.

### 4. The layman's catalogue, and what to do with contempt

The chapter then reports what the layman thinks politics *is*: **"public meetings, processions, slogans, demonstrations, demands, strikes, tear-gas, lathi-charge as well as elections accompanied by false promises and false reports on their fulfilment."** Hence the verdict that politics is **"the last resort of scoundrels"**; hence **Ernest Benn**'s sardonic definition — politics is **"the art of looking for trouble, finding it where it exists or not, diagnosing it wrongly, and applying the wrong remedy"**; hence politics is **"sometimes condemned as a 'dirty game'"**, students **"are advised to keep away from politics"**, and **"judges and other intellectuals are expected to keep themselves above politics."**

❗ Both owners carry only *dirty game*. The full catalogue matters for three reasons. First, its **idiom is Indian** — *lathi-charge*, *tear-gas*, *processions*, *false reports on their fulfilment* — so it can be used in an Indian-context stem with no imported fact and no dating risk. Second, Benn's sentence is the chapter's only quotable **epigram**, and an epigram deployed and then dismantled is worth more than a paragraph of denial. ⚠️ Benn's wording differs from versions circulating elsewhere; this edition uses the chapter's form when Gauba is cited and ❌ makes no attempt to adjudicate the original. Third, and most importantly, the chapter supplies the clause that converts the whole band from a complaint into an argument: ⭐ **"Such vague assumptions are the reason why politics often gets dubbed the 'last resort of scoundrels'."**

⚠️ Read that clause carefully. The contempt is not refuted; it is **explained**. It is the predictable consequence of a vague definition, which means the cure for the contempt is the same as the cure for the vagueness. That is also the chapter's own statement of method, and it is worth reproducing where a stem asks why definitional work is needed at all: ⭐ **"For a systematic study of politics it is essential to dispel such confusion and to lift politics from the arena of vague impressions to the level of scientific precision."**

### 5. The old programme and the new thesis — the hinge of the chapter

The word derives from **'polis'**, the ancient Greek city-state, and **"the activities of citizens in their role as members and operators of the state were termed by ancient Greek thinkers as 'politics'."** So **"from the very beginning, 'politics' came to be associated with the 'state'"**, and consequently **"traditional writers have considered 'political science' as the 'science of the state', and have devoted themselves: (a) to a study of the institutions of the state; and (b) to developing ideas concerning the nature of a perfect state."**

❗ Reproduce the **two limbs**. An answer that cannot state the old programme cannot earn marks for displacing it, and candidates routinely state limb (a) and drop limb (b) — which is the **normative** limb, and the one that explains why classical political theory looks the way it does.

Then the thesis sentence of the entire chapter: **"However, modern writers have increasingly recognized that 'politics' does not operate strictly within the framework of an institutional set-up but permeates the entire social fabric. Accordingly, 'politics' is now treated as a social process rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the state."**

⭐ The clause that does the work is **"permeates the entire social fabric"**. The basic owner paraphrases the turn; neither owner reproduces this clause, and it is the clause that **licenses political analysis outside government** — which is to say, it licenses Lessons 2 to 11. ⚠️ But note at once what it does **not** license: the chapter immediately constrains it at p.96 with an explicit exclusion, taught at Lesson 2, so that the process turn does not collapse into "everything is political". ➡️ The Greek-narrowing argument itself, Wolin's scope criterion, the public/private lists and the override clause belong to **Topic 01**, `basic\01_Nature-and-Significance-of-Political-Theory.md` (Gauba Ch.1, pp.15–16), whose released Mains Lesson 1 already owns them; this topic takes only the one-line derivation and the two-limbed programme. ➡️ Liberalism's tenets and Locke's doctrine are **Topic 03**'s.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

⚠️ Framed as **supplementary conceptual support only**, as conceptual transfer and never as a claimed route. ❗ No General Studies or Essay previous-year question exists for Political Theory in any of the eleven routing ledgers, and none is claimed here; the actual owners are named at each point.

- **GS-II.** Where a stem concerns the reach of governance beyond formal institutions — administrative behaviour, informal power, the gap between a rule and its working — the process turn supplies the licensing sentence in one line: political analysis applies because politics **"permeates the entire social fabric"** rather than being confined to an institutional set-up. ⚠️ Every institutional, constitutional or scheme-level claim in such an answer must come from the `Polity\…` and `Governance\…` owners and from independently verified preparation; ❌ nothing institutional is asserted from this topic.
- **GS-IV.** Ball's problem [2] is a usable integrity point: treating *politics* as synonymous with the intrigues of its worst practitioners is a **category error**, and an officer who writes off the political process on that basis has confused an activity with a sample of its conduct. ⚠️ Codes, case material and the printed Ethics stems are owned by the `Ethics\…` files.
- **Essay.** On any prompt about public life, cynicism or the standing of democratic activity, the discard band plus its explanatory clause is a complete opening movement: list the images, name the contempt, then show with Gauba's own clause that the contempt is the **predictable consequence of a vague definition** rather than an observation about politics. ⚠️ Essay prompt scoping is owned by `Essay\advanced\03_Issue-Based-Prompt-Scoping.md`; ❌ no Essay previous-year prompt is claimed for this topic.

**Answer framework — the opening that buys three marks.** One clause disambiguating the term (*politics as an activity, not as the discipline*) → one sentence fixing the object (*always concerned with a particular type of human activity*) → one sentence naming **both** of Ball's problems and attaching the correction to each → one clause stating the turn (*a social process rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the state*) → one clause announcing the structure the body will follow. About sixty words, and it has already disambiguated, fixed the object, cleared two errors, stated the thesis and promised a shape.

Revision notes:

- ⭐ The term is applied **"to a particular social phenomenon as well as to a systematic study of that phenomenon"** — disambiguate in the first clause.
- Aristotle used *Politics* **"to indicate a distinct branch of study"**; today the term is **"often used synonymously with 'political science'."**
- ⭐ The object, fixed in six words: our subject **"is always concerned with a particular type of human activity."**
- **Evidence for interchangeable usage**: University of **Bombay** — Civics and Politics; University of **Delhi** — Political Science; **Gokhale Institute** of Politics and Economics; **London School** of Economics and Political Science. ❌ Nothing else may be asserted about any of them.
- ⭐ **Ball's two problems** (*Modern Politics and Government*; 1988): [1] politics is **"only concerned with the public sector, with parliaments, elections, cabinets"**; [2] **"confusing politics solely with party politics ... a distaste with the intrigues and tricks of party politicians seeking power."** Two errors, two cures.
- The layman's catalogue: **"public meetings, processions, slogans, demonstrations, demands, strikes, tear-gas, lathi-charge"** plus elections with **"false promises and false reports on their fulfilment."** Indian in idiom, costless to deploy.
- The two condemnations: **"the last resort of scoundrels"** and **"a dirty game"**; students advised to keep away; judges and intellectuals **"expected to keep themselves above politics."**
- **Ernest Benn**: politics is **"the art of looking for trouble, finding it where it exists or not, diagnosing it wrongly, and applying the wrong remedy."** ⚠️ Chapter's wording used; the original is not adjudicated here.
- ⭐ The clause that turns insult into argument: **"Such vague assumptions are the reason why politics often gets dubbed"** — the contempt is an **explanandum**.
- The method clause: **"it is essential to dispel such confusion and to lift politics from the arena of vague impressions to the level of scientific precision."**
- ***polis*** = the ancient Greek city-state; citizens **"in their role as members and operators of the state"**; hence **"political science"** as **"the science of the state"**, with **two** limbs — (a) **the institutions of the state**, (b) **the nature of a perfect state**.
- ⭐ The thesis sentence: politics **"permeates the entire social fabric"**, and is **"now treated as a social process rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the state."** ⚠️ The chapter constrains this at Lesson 2; it is not a claim that everything is political.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate opens: "Politics is the study of the state and its institutions. Unfortunately, it has become a dirty game." Using only the chapter's first two pages, name three things wrong with that opening and state what each correction buys.

**Model answer:** First, the opening has not **disambiguated**. The chapter's own first sentence is that the term is applied **"to a particular social phenomenon as well as to a systematic study of that phenomenon"**, so an answer must say which sense is in play; and in either sense the object is the same — the subject **"is always concerned with a particular type of human activity."** Buying: it converts a question about a syllabus into a question about an activity, which is what the stem actually asks. Second, the opening reproduces **Ball's problem [1]** — the assumption that politics is **"only concerned with the public sector, with parliaments, elections, cabinets, and has little relevance to other spheres of human activity."** The chapter's correction is its thesis sentence: politics **"does not operate strictly within the framework of an institutional set-up but permeates the entire social fabric"**, and is **"now treated as a social process rather than an aggregate of the formal institutions of the state."** Buying: the licence to analyse politically outside government. Third, the *dirty game* clause is used as a **verdict** when the chapter uses it as a **symptom**: it reports the condemnation and then explains it — **"such vague assumptions are the reason why politics often gets dubbed the 'last resort of scoundrels'."** Buying: the cynicism becomes something the answer explains rather than something it concedes.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not read the process turn as the claim that everything is political. The chapter restricts it two pages later with an explicit conditional exclusion — the politics of a church, a university or a factory is excluded **"as long as"** it is **"confined to a single organization with little effect on public life."** An answer that takes *permeates the entire social fabric* as licence to call every disagreement political has lost the test the next lesson supplies, and with it the ability to answer the question *which* conflicts are political.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Explain why Gauba begins an account of the nature of politics with the layman's image of it, rather than with a definition. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** **Because the popular image is the obstacle, and it has a stated cause.** Gauba reports, through **Alan Ball** (*Modern Politics and Government*; 1988), **two** distinct misconceptions: that politics is **"only concerned with the public sector, with parliaments, elections, cabinets"**, and the **"confusing [of] politics solely with party politics"** and its **"intrigues and tricks."** He then lists what the layman actually pictures — **processions, strikes, tear-gas, lathi-charge**, elections with **"false promises"** — and supplies the causal clause: **"such vague assumptions are the reason why politics often gets dubbed the 'last resort of scoundrels'."** ⚠️ The contempt is therefore an **effect of imprecision**, not evidence about politics, which is why the chapter's stated method is to **"lift politics from the arena of vague impressions to the level of scientific precision."** A definition offered first would have been accepted as a slogan; offered after the obstacle is named, it answers something.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 4 + 3 + 2 + 1):** 4 for reproducing **both** of Ball's problems as separable errors with the correct correction attached to each — institutional narrowing cured by the process turn, partisan reduction cured by fixing the object as an activity; 3 for the causal clause (*such vague assumptions are the reason why*) used as the argument rather than the catalogue quoted as decoration; 2 for the stated method (*from vague impressions to scientific precision*) given as the reason for the chapter's order; 1 for directive fidelity, since **Explain** demands the ground and not a restatement. ❌ Cap at 5 if the two problems are fused into one; deduct 2 if *dirty game* is endorsed rather than explained.

## Lesson 2 — The political situation: three definitions, one counter-example, two defects

Progress: 2 / 11 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: Ball, Wasby and Miller as a graded sequence of definitions; the chapter's own one-sentence synthesis; the nursery counter-example and its two stated defects; the four-part public-conflict criterion with the universal-solution requirement; the conditional organisational exclusion; and war as the symptom of a failed political solution

Book context: Queried — basic owner §§3, 5, 9; advanced owner §§2-3; Gauba PDF **pp.95-96** read directly for the three definitions with their titles and dates, Ball's nursery example, the two defects named in order, the public-conflict criterion, the church/university/factory clause and the six-item method list.
CA search: ❌ **Not run at this lesson.** Current affairs is not searched lesson by lesson on this topic; the single bounded pass is reported at **Lesson 9**.
CA found: Nothing is carried here. ⚠️ No post-2009 scheme, statute, judgment, index figure or statistic enters this lesson, and none is needed: this is a logical test, not a current question.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

❗ **This is the only place in the whole subject where Topic 07 owns its material outright.** No sibling Mains edition teaches Gauba Ch.3, so none holds the nursery counter-example, its two stated defects, the major-groups criterion or the war exclusion. Topic 01's released Lesson 1 holds a *different* argument from Ch.1 pp.15–16 — Wolin's scope criterion, the public and private lists and the override clause — which is about the **scope of political theory**. ➡️ That is Topic 01's and is not touched here. What this lesson supplies is an **operational test for whether a particular situation is political at all**, which is a different object and is uncontested. ⚠️ It is also the only apparatus in this topic that a candidate can **run against a novel case in the examination hall**, which is why it is taught as a gate rather than as a definition.

```text
  VISUAL 2 - THE QUALIFICATION GATE: four tests, and the case that fails
  ======================================================== (PDF pp.95-96)

  ENTRY: ALAN BALL'S NURSERY CASE
  'TWO CHILDREN IN A NURSERY WITH ONE TOY WHICH THEY BOTH WANT AT THE
   SAME TIME PRESENT A POLITICAL SITUATION ... THE TWO CHILDREN COULD
   RESORT TO VIOLENCE, WITH THE STRONGER CLAIMING THE TOY, OR THE
   MOTHER COULD APPEAR AND USE HER STRONGER POSITION TO ARBITRATE'
      |                                    [side entry]
      |                         HUSBAND + WIFE ON THE FAMILY BUDGET
      |                                    |
      v                                    v
  +==================================================================+
  | GATE 1   Does the conflict arise at a 'PUBLIC' LEVEL?            |
  +==================================================================+
      |  NURSERY: NO -- 'A PRIVATE CONFLICT BETWEEN TWO CHILDREN
      |            WHICH DOES NOT QUALIFY'       <== **DEFECT 1**
      |  BUDGET : NO -- 'DOES NOT PRESENT A POLITICAL SITUATION'
      |  (both are rejected here and travel no further)
      v
  +==================================================================+
  | GATE 2   Are TWO OR MORE MAJOR GROUPS involved?                  |
  |          -- in ANY social setting: local / regional / national /  |
  |             international                                         |
  |          -- on ANY plane: economic / cultural / linguistic /      |
  |             religious / ethnic                                    |
  +==================================================================+
      v
  +==================================================================+
  | GATE 3   Does it demand a 'UNIVERSAL' SOLUTION, not a private    |
  |          one?  ('public' issues, not private issues)             |
  +==================================================================+
      |
      |   [CONDITIONAL SIDE-GATE] politics of CHURCH / UNIVERSITY /
      |   FACTORY passes 2, and fails 3 ONLY on the condition:
      |   'AS LONG AS THIS SO-CALLED POLITICS IS CONFINED TO A SINGLE
      |    ORGANIZATION WITH LITTLE EFFECT ON PUBLIC LIFE'
      |   >> the exclusion is CONDITIONAL, never categorical
      v
  +==================================================================+
  | GATE 4   Is it settled by a method SHORT OF WAR?                 |
  |   admitted: NEGOTIATION | PERSUASION | ARBITRATION | COMPROMISE  |
  |             | PRESSURE | COUNTING OF VOTES                       |
  |   and: 'THE SOLUTION IS SUPPOSED TO BE ACCEPTABLE TO THE PARTIES' |
  +==================================================================+
      |  NURSERY: NO -- the example 'CONTEMPLATES RESORT TO VIOLENCE
      |            AS ONE OF THE POSSIBLE METHODS'   <== **DEFECT 2**
      |            'RESORT TO VIOLENCE OR WAR DOES NOT QUALIFY FOR A
      |             POLITICAL SOLUTION'
      v
  === PASS ===> A POLITICAL SITUATION

  VERDICT ON THE ENTRY CASE: 'THIS EXAMPLE SUFFERS FROM TWO DEFECTS.'
  It fails at GATE 1 and at GATE 4 -- and the chapter counts them.
  WAR IS NOT EXCLUDED AS IMPOSSIBLE: 'SOME POLITICAL DISPUTES DO
  CULMINATE IN WAR, BUT WAR IS A SYMPTOM OF THE FAILURE OF A POLITICAL
  SOLUTION.'
```

*A worked counter-example run through four gates, with the chapter's own error-count attached. The two defects are not this session's criticism of Ball; they are Gauba's, stated in his own words and numbered by him.*

### 1. Three definitions, read as a sequence rather than as a list

The chapter gives three, and ❗ **not one of the three authorities is named in either owner**, with none of their titles and none of their dates. They are also **graded**: each adds something the previous one lacked, and reproducing the sequence shows a definition being **built**, which earns more than any single definition can.

| # | Authority, work and date | What it says | What it adds |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Alan Ball**, *Modern Politics and Government*; 1988 | political activity **"involves disagreements and the reconciliation of those disagreements"**; hence **"the essence of the political situation"** is **"conflict and resolution of that conflict"** | **conflict**, as the raw material |
| 2 | **Stephen L. Wasby**, *Political Science—The Discipline and its Dimensions: An Introduction*; 1972 | **"Where there is politics, it is said, there is controversy; where there are issues, there is politics. Where no controversy exists, where no issues are being debated, politics does not exist."** | the **biconditional** — not merely that politics involves controversy, but that **without controversy there is no politics** |
| 3 | **J.D.B. Miller**, *The Nature of Politics*; 1962, block-quoted | political activity **"arises out of disagreement, and it is concerned with the use of government to resolve conflict in the direction of change or in the prevention of change"**; it demands **"some initial disagreement between parties or persons, and the presence of government as a means of resolving the disagreement in some direction"** | **government**, as a necessary element of the resolution |

⚠️ Wasby's second sentence is the one candidates drop and the one that does the work. It is a **negative existence claim**: no controversy, no politics. That is what makes it possible to say of a given situation that it is *not* political — which is the whole purpose of the lesson. And Miller adds the element that keeps the process turn of Lesson 1 from becoming boundless: resolution must involve **the use of government**, so a social process that settles itself without any reference to government is not, on this account, politics.

The chapter then compresses all three into its own sentence: ⭐ **"In a nutshell, the political process postulates the existence of a disagreement or conflict, and efforts for the resolution of that conflict through the authority of government."** ⚠️ Note **"through the authority of government"**. That clause matters again at Lesson 3, where Easton's claim that authoritative allocation is unavoidable must not be read as a claim that politics is everywhere **informal**.

### 2. The negation sentence, and why it is the best transition in the chapter

Immediately after the synthesis comes ⭐ **"Thus, every situation involving a conflict and efforts for its resolution cannot be called a 'political situation'."**

❗ This single sentence is the licence for everything that follows, and it is the best available transition line into the counter-example. It says explicitly that *conflict plus resolution* is **not sufficient** — which means a test is owed, and the chapter is about to supply one by working through a case that fails.

### 3. The nursery case, and its two stated defects

Ball's example, reproduced: **"Two children in a nursery with one toy which they both want at the same time present a political situation... The two children could resort to violence, with the stronger claiming the toy, or the mother could appear and use her stronger position to arbitrate between the quarrelling children."**

Gauba's verdict is flat: **"This example suffers from two defects."**

**Defect 1 — it is private.** The example **"refers to a 'private' conflict between two children which does not qualify for being a political situation."** The chapter adds a parallel case at once: **"Similarly, a disagreement between husband and wife on the family budget does not present a political situation."**

**Defect 2 — it admits violence as a method.** Ball's example **"contemplates 'resort to violence' as one of the possible methods of conflict-resolution. But, properly speaking, resort to violence or war does not qualify for a political solution."**

❗ **This structure is absent from both owners in every part, and it is the single best thing in the chapter for Mains purposes.** It is a **worked counter-example with a stated error count** — the examiner's own favourite form — and it supplies the criterion **by negation**, which is far more durable under pressure than a positive definition. A candidate who has to produce a definition of politics from memory may produce a vague one; a candidate who remembers *two children, one toy, two defects* can rebuild the criterion from the failure.

⚠️ One point of fairness that is also a point of marks: the chapter is **criticising a named authority it has just used**. Ball supplied definition [1] and is corrected on his own example two paragraphs later. Noting that in half a line shows an examiner that the sequence has been read as an argument rather than copied as a list.

### 4. The criterion, which has four separable parts and a requirement

Having rejected the case, the chapter states the test: only that conflict is properly political **"which arises at a 'public' level, i.e. in which two or more major groups are involved in any social setting, whether local, regional, national or international, or at the economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic plane. In other words, a political conflict is always concerned with 'public' issues, not private issues; it demands a 'universal' solution, not a private one."**

❗ Four tests and one requirement, in one printed sentence, and the owners give **"public conflict"** with no test at all:

1. the conflict arises at a **public level**;
2. **two or more major groups** are involved — not two individuals, and not one group alone;
3. the **setting** may be anything from **local** to **international** — so scale is not a criterion;
4. the **plane** may be **economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic** — so subject matter is not a criterion either;
5. and the conflict **demands a 'universal' solution, not a private one**.

⚠️ Points 3 and 4 are the ones most often lost, and they are **permissive** rather than restrictive: they widen the test by saying that neither scale nor subject matter disqualifies. What disqualifies is privacy and the absence of major groups. ⭐ Run this against any novel case in a stem and an answer can justify, in two lines, why a given dispute is or is not a political one.

### 5. The organisational exclusion — and its conditional

**"In common parlance, we talk of the politics of church, university or a factory. But as long as this so-called politics is confined to a single organization with little effect on public life, it cannot be considered the proper subject of study in political science."**

❗ This is the chapter's own limiting case, and it is what stops the process turn of Lesson 1 from collapsing into "everything is political". ⚠️ **But read the qualifier.** The exclusion is governed by **"as long as"** and by **"with little effect on public life"** — so it is **conditional, not categorical**. Organisational politics is excluded *while* it stays confined and inconsequential; the implication, which the chapter leaves standing, is that organisational politics with a substantial effect on public life would **not** be excluded. ❌ Never state the exclusion categorically. ⭐ A *critically examine* directive rewards exactly this kind of reading, because it shows the candidate has noticed that the chapter has built a threshold rather than a wall. 📝 The scan prints `co-called` for *so-called*; the intended word is reproduced and the raw form is disclosed as normalisation N3.

### 6. War, the method list, and the acceptability condition

**"No doubt, some political disputes do culminate in war, but war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution."** By contrast, **"a political solution emanates from negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure, counting of votes or any similar tactic short of war. In any case, the solution is supposed to be acceptable to the parties involved in the dispute."**

⚠️ The basic owner carries the war claim and drops both the **six-item method list** and the **acceptability** condition. The list is what makes the claim operational — it says what a political method positively *is*, not merely what it is not. ❗ And note the composition of the list: *counting of votes* sits beside **pressure**, which is the chapter's quiet concession that political method is not all deliberation. An answer that reproduces the list without noticing that concession has read it as a list; an answer that notices it has read it as an argument.

⚠️ Note also the precise form of the war claim. War is not said to be outside politics altogether — some political disputes **do** culminate in it. It is said to be a **symptom of failure**, which is a claim about what war indicates, not a claim that war never happens politically. ❌ Do not render it as "war is not politics".

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

⚠️ Conceptual transfer only. ❗ No General Studies or Essay previous-year question exists for this subject, and none is claimed; actual owners are named at each point.

- **GS-II.** Wherever a stem asks whether a particular dispute belongs to the public domain — a demand pressed by a sectional association, a grievance confined to one institution, a controversy that has outgrown its origin — the four gates give a defensible answer rather than an assertion, and the conditional clause (*with little effect on public life*) is the tool that handles the borderline case. ⚠️ Institutions, statutes and commissions belong to the `Polity\…` and `Governance\…` owners; ❌ nothing institutional is asserted here.
- **GS-IV.** The private/public conversion test is directly usable in administrative ethics: a complaint about an individual superior is private; the same complaint framed as a question about the **respective claims of categories of staff, demanding a solution at the organisational or public level**, is of a different order and calls for a different remedy. ⚠️ That is the logical form only; the ethical doctrine and the printed Ethics stems are the `Ethics\…` owners'.
- **Essay.** On any prompt about conflict, dissent or civil peace, Gauba's formulation is an exceptionally clean middle paragraph: **"war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution"**, followed by the six admitted methods, followed by the acceptability condition. It concedes that disputes can end in violence while denying that violence is a political settlement. ⚠️ Prompt scoping is `Essay\advanced\03_Issue-Based-Prompt-Scoping.md`'s.

**Answer framework — the paragraph that converts a definition into a test.** State the synthesis in one sentence (*disagreement, plus efforts at resolution, through the authority of government*) → state the negation (*not every conflict-and-resolution is political*) → run the four gates on the case the stem actually gives, naming **two or more major groups** and the **universal solution** requirement explicitly → add the conditional exclusion if the case is organisational → close with the war clause if the stem concerns violence. This is one of the very few paragraphs in this topic that can be **applied** rather than merely reproduced, which is why it is worth the practice.

Revision notes:

- **Ball 1988** (*Modern Politics and Government*): political activity **"involves disagreements and the reconciliation of those disagreements"**; the essence is **"conflict and resolution of that conflict."**
- ⭐ **Wasby 1972** (*Political Science—The Discipline and its Dimensions*): **"Where no controversy exists, where no issues are being debated, politics does not exist."** The **biconditional** is the quotable half.
- **Miller 1962** (*The Nature of Politics*): disagreement **plus** **"the presence of government as a means of resolving the disagreement in some direction."**
- ⭐ The chapter's synthesis: the political process **"postulates the existence of a disagreement or conflict, and efforts for the resolution of that conflict through the authority of government."**
- ⭐ The negation: **"every situation involving a conflict and efforts for its resolution cannot be called a 'political situation'."**
- **The nursery case** — two children, one toy; violence or the mother's arbitration — and Gauba's verdict: **"This example suffers from two defects."**
- **Defect 1**: a **"'private' conflict between two children which does not qualify"**; likewise **"a disagreement between husband and wife on the family budget."**
- **Defect 2**: it **"contemplates 'resort to violence' as one of the possible methods"**, but **"resort to violence or war does not qualify for a political solution."**
- ⭐ **The four-part criterion**: a **"'public' level"**; **"two or more major groups"**; any setting, **"local, regional, national or international"**; any plane, **"economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic"** — plus the demand for a **"'universal' solution, not a private one."**
- ⚠️ **The conditional exclusion**: church, university, factory — excluded **"as long as"** confined to a single organisation **"with little effect on public life."** ❌ Never categorical.
- **War**: **"some political disputes do culminate in war, but war is a symptom of the failure of a political solution."**
- **The six admitted methods**: **negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure, counting of votes** — **"or any similar tactic short of war"** — and **"the solution is supposed to be acceptable to the parties involved."** ⚠️ *Pressure* beside *counting of votes* is a concession worth one line.

### Concept check

**Question:** A stem gives you a prolonged dispute inside one large university over the allocation of staff quarters, which has begun to attract wider attention. Using only this chapter's apparatus, decide whether it is a political situation — and show which clause decides it.

**Model answer:** Run the chapter's gates. **Gate 1, the public level.** The dispute is not private in the nursery sense: it is not a quarrel between two persons but a claim about allocation among categories of people, so it is not disposed of by Defect 1, which excludes **"a 'private' conflict between two children"** and **"a disagreement between husband and wife on the family budget."** **Gate 2, major groups.** The test is **"two or more major groups ... in any social setting, whether local, regional, national or international, or at the economic, cultural, linguistic, religious or ethnic plane"** — scale and subject matter do **not** disqualify, so a single campus is admissible provided the parties are organised categories rather than individuals. **Gate 3, universality.** A political conflict **"demands a 'universal' solution, not a private one"**; a rule governing allocation, rather than a settlement for one claimant, meets that. **Gate 4, method.** It proceeds by negotiation and pressure, both of which the chapter admits as **"tactic[s] short of war."** ❗ **The deciding clause is the organisational one**, and it is conditional: such politics is excluded **"as long as this so-called politics is confined to a single organization with little effect on public life."** Because the dispute has begun to reach beyond the organisation, the condition that produces the exclusion is weakening — so the correct answer is **graded**: political to the extent that its effect on public life ceases to be little.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not state the organisational exclusion as a flat rule that the politics of a university is never political. The chapter governs the exclusion by **"as long as"** and by **"with little effect on public life"**, and a conditional stated as a categorical is the standard way this clause is misquoted. The same conditional is what keeps the process turn of Lesson 1 coherent, so flattening it costs twice.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Examine Gauba's claim that Alan Ball's nursery example fails to present a political situation. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** **The claim holds, and the chapter supplies both reasons and counts them.** Ball's case — two children, one toy, settled by violence or by the mother — is said to **"suffer from two defects."** **Defect 1:** it is **"a 'private' conflict between two children which does not qualify"**, since a political conflict must arise at a **"'public' level"** with **"two or more major groups"** involved and must demand **"a 'universal' solution, not a private one."** **Defect 2:** it **"contemplates 'resort to violence' as one of the possible methods"**, whereas a political solution **"emanates from negotiation, persuasion, arbitration, compromise, pressure, counting of votes or any similar tactic short of war."** ⚠️ **Qualification:** the second defect does not deny that violence follows politics — **"some political disputes do culminate in war"** — only that war is **"a symptom of the failure of a political solution."** The example fails twice.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 3 + 2 + 2):** 3 for reproducing Defect 1 with the public-level test attached, including **two or more major groups** and the **universal solution** requirement rather than a bare "it is private"; 3 for Defect 2 with the six-item method list, since the list is what makes the exclusion operational; 2 for the qualification that war is not excluded as an outcome but classified as a **symptom of failure**; 2 for treating the verdict as **Gauba's own numbered criticism of an authority he has just cited**, not as the candidate's improvisation. ❌ Deduct 3 if only one defect is given; ❌ cap at 6 if the war clause is rendered as "war is not politics".

## Lesson 3 — Easton and Roberts: what makes a decision bind, and why politics cannot be abolished

Progress: 3 / 11 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Easton's three terms each defined in turn, the policy/web-of-decisions/selection descent, the *must or ought* pair against brute force, the unavoidability claim block-quoted, the chapter marking its own argumentative progress, Roberts's four-part definition with its hedges, and the politics/economics demarcation

Lesson 2 established **when** a situation is political. This lesson supplies the answer to the question Lesson 2 ends on — *how is an acceptable solution made possible?* — and it contains the single most powerful sentence in pp.94–107. ❗ Both owners give Easton's phrase **"authoritative allocation of values"** as a slogan and reproduce **none of its three definitions**, neither his book nor its date, neither *web of decisions* nor *selection among alternatives*, and ❗ **not his unavoidability claim**, which is held by no sibling edition either. Four printed sentences convert a tag into a theory; one block quotation converts the theory into a universality thesis that is directly usable where a stem asks whether political authority can be dispensed with.

```text
    THAT ARE TO BE PURSUED, THAT IS FOR DECIDING WHO IS TO GET WHAT
```
