          TEMPORALLY AND CONCERNING RESOURCE ALLOCATION, AND
          IMPLEMENTED'
    (iii) 'INVOLVES BOTH COOPERATION AND THE RESOLUTION OF CONFLICT,
          BY MEANS OF THE EXERCISE OF POLITICAL AUTHORITY, AND IF
          NECESSARY, COERCION'
    (iv)  'DISTINGUISHED FROM OTHER SOCIAL PROCESSES BY ITS CONCERN
          WITH THE PUBLIC GOALS OF THE SOCIETY'
    hedges to keep: 'USUALLY involves the activities of groups ...
    including SOMETIMES groups of a specifically political type'
    BOUNDARY: economics 'MAY BE CONCERNED WITH PUBLIC OR PRIVATE
    ALLOCATION'; politics is 'EXCLUSIVELY' concerned with the PUBLIC.
```

*Three columns, one descent, one band and one margin strip. The triptych is the theory; the band is the thesis; the strip is the bridge the chapter builds to cooperation three sections before the communitarians arrive.*

### 1. The three terms, each with its printed definition

The chapter is explicit that the **"short but compact definition involves three terms which must be understood clearly."**

- **Values** — **"things considered valuable, whether they be spiritual or material."** ⚠️ The disjunction is load-bearing. If values were material only, the whole of the politics of status, recognition, language and religious practice would fall outside the definition; the chapter's own value list at Lesson 4 includes **rectitude** and **deference**, which are not material at all.
- **Allocation** — **"distribution of these things to various individuals or groups"**, accomplished **"through policy which consists of a 'web of decisions'"**, where a decision **"denotes the 'selection among alternatives'"**, and **"policy implies arriving at a decision as well as its implementation."** ❗ The phrase ⭐ **"web of decisions"** is what distinguishes a **policy** from a single order. A policy is a structure of connected choices with implementation attached, which is why policy can be incoherent, can be subverted in execution, and can be analysed without analysing any one decision.
- **Authoritative** — **"a policy is authoritative when the people to whom it is intended to apply or who are affected by it consider that they must or ought to obey it."** ❗ ⭐ The pair ***must or ought*** is the whole of the legitimacy question in four words: *must* is the language of prudence and consequence, *ought* the language of obligation, and the chapter's definition admits **either**. The sentence that follows is the one most worth carrying verbatim: **"authority does not signify the use of brute force; it denotes the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance from its subjects for a particular decision or course of action."**

⚠️ **Note the hedge in that last clause.** It is not *willing compliance* but **"more-or-less willing compliance"**. ❗ And note what the two halves of the *must or ought* pair will do to each other at Lesson 5, where the chapter lists **"purely from fear of consequences"** among the grounds on which a policy may be accepted as authoritative. ➡️ The doctrine of authority, Weber's typology, the faces of power and legitimacy theory are **Topic 15**'s, `basic\15_Power-Authority-and-Legitimacy.md`, whose released Mains Lesson 2 owns the formula that converts power into authority; this topic cites the two clauses **as Easton's usage** and stops. ➡️ Theories of political obligation, consent and the grounds of obedience are **Topic 14**'s, whose released Lesson 1 owns the grounds-of-obedience ladder.

### 2. The chapter marking its own progress, and why reproducing that earns a structure mark

Before the block quotation the chapter states the universality claim in its own voice: ⭐ **"politics as the 'authoritative allocation of values' represents a universal social phenomenon."** ⚠️ Useful where the full quotation will not fit the word budget.

And after it, the chapter says what it has just done: ⭐ **"Easton's observations add a new dimension to our earlier formulation regarding the nature of politics. It brings in the element of authoritativeness to the process of conflict-resolution."**

❗ Reproducing that sentence — or paraphrasing its function in half a line — demonstrates that the candidate is tracking a **developing definition** rather than listing definitions, and that is a structure mark in its own right. The sequence so far is: conflict (Ball) → the biconditional (Wasby) → government (Miller) → **authoritativeness** (Easton) → cooperation and the public-goals boundary (Roberts). Five steps, each adding one element, each named and dated.

### 3. The unavoidability claim — the most powerful sentence in the chapter

Block-quoted from Easton himself: **"Every society provides some mechanisms, however rudimentary they may be, for authoritatively resolving differences about the ends that are to be pursued, that is for deciding who is to get what there is of desirable things. An authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable."**

❗ **Absent from both owners, and uncontested across the whole subject.** Three things make it decisive.

First, it is a **universality thesis**, not a generalisation: politics is not a feature of some societies but of **every** society, and the clause **"however rudimentary they may be"** blocks the obvious reply that simple societies have no politics.

Second, it is the direct, named, quotable answer to the second clause of a stem asking whether political authority can be dispensed with completely. ⚠️ **2021 Philosophy Optional Q3(a) [20]** — *"Discuss anarchism as a political ideology. Is it possible to dispense with political authority completely? Give reasons for your answer."* — is owned by `Philosophy\paper-2\socio-political\Political-Ideologies.md`, and **Topic 20's released Mains edition reproduces the stem**. ❗ Topic 07's **one move**, which neither holds, is this sentence: the chapter asserts that authoritative allocation is **unavoidable**, with a reason — every society must decide **"who is to get what there is of desirable things"** — so the burden on the anarchist is to show not that the state is dispensable but that the **allocative function** is. ❌ Anarchism as a doctrine is not taught here, and the stem is not presented as this topic's to answer.

Third, it sets up the chapter's own most productive contradiction. ⚠️ At p.104 the chapter will report, without comment, that **"the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself."** Those two sentences cannot both be true as stated. ⚠️ **The chapter never stages the collision.** This edition stages it deliberately at Lesson 8 and again at Lesson 10, and marks the staging as this session's rather than the chapter's.

### 4. Roberts's definition, in its four parts, with its hedges intact

**Geoffrey K. Roberts** (*A Dictionary of Political Analysis*; 1971) is named, titled and dated in neither owner. His definition has four separable parts, set out in the margin strip of the visual above, and two features make it worth the space.

❗ **It is the only place in pp.94–107 where cooperation is placed alongside conflict-resolution before the communitarian view arrives.** Roberts holds that the political process **"involves both cooperation and the resolution of conflict, by means of the exercise of political authority, and if necessary, coercion"**, and the chapter introduces him explicitly as **"Taking a liberal view of politics"**. That is a bridge the chapter builds and both owners lose: the communitarian claim that politics is cooperative is not an entirely external intrusion at p.104, because a **liberal** definition already admits cooperation at p.97. ⭐ One line to that effect materially improves any answer comparing the three views, because it stops the comparison becoming a caricature in which liberals see only conflict.

⚠️ **Reproduce the hedges.** Roberts says politics **"usually involves the activities of groups of various kinds, including sometimes groups of a specifically political type, such as political parties."** *Usually* and *sometimes* are precisely what stop the group account from becoming the claim that politics is **always** party politics — i.e. from committing Ball's problem [2] from Lesson 1. ❌ Dropping either hedge reintroduces the error the chapter opened by removing.

### 5. The politics/economics boundary, in one clause

**"Whereas 'economics' may be concerned with public or private allocation of resources, 'politics' is exclusively concerned with the 'public' allocation of resources in pursuance of 'public goals'."**

❗ A one-line disciplinary demarcation with an **"exclusively"** in it, absent from both owners. Its second function is anticipatory: it states what the **liberal** account takes the difference between politics and economics to be, which is exactly the claim the Marxist view of Lesson 8 will deny when it makes conflicting economic interests **"the motive force behind all politics."** ⚠️ Teaching the boundary here makes that later denial legible as a disagreement rather than as a change of subject. ➡️ Economic doctrine, development and growth belong to `Philosophy\paper-2\socio-political\Development-Social-Progress.md` and the Economy owners; ❌ nothing economic is asserted from this topic.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

⚠️ Conceptual transfer only; ❗ no General Studies or Essay route exists for this subject and none is claimed.

- **GS-II.** Easton's three terms give a precise vocabulary for any stem about why a contested public decision is nonetheless obeyed: the decision is part of a **policy**, which is a **web of decisions** with implementation attached, and it binds because those affected consider that they **must or ought** to obey — a formulation that separates compliance from agreement without invoking either consent theory or coercion theory. ⚠️ Institutional detail, articles and schemes belong to the `Polity\…` and `Governance\…` owners.
- **GS-IV.** The *must or ought* pair is an unusually clean instrument for an administrative-ethics stem: an order complied with only because of **"fear of consequences"** is authoritative on Easton's account but is not obeyed for the reason the official would want, and the gap between *must* and *ought* is exactly where integrity questions live. ⚠️ The ethical doctrine and the printed stems are the `Ethics\…` owners'.
- **Essay.** The unavoidability claim is a strong, quotable pivot for any prompt about order, anarchy or the limits of self-regulation: every society, **"however rudimentary"**, has mechanisms for deciding **"who is to get what there is of desirable things."** ⚠️ Prompt scoping is `Essay\advanced\03_…`'s; ❌ no Essay prompt is claimed.

**Answer framework — the apparatus paragraph.** Name the formula and date it (*Easton, 1953*) → unpack **all three** terms in one sentence each, keeping the *spiritual or material* disjunction and the *web of decisions* descent → give the *must or ought* pair with the brute-force denial → close the paragraph on the unavoidability quotation if the stem concerns whether politics can be dispensed with, or on the public-goals boundary if the stem concerns what distinguishes politics from economics or administration. ⚠️ Roberts is the **optional fifth sentence**, used when the answer needs cooperation on the table early.

Revision notes:

- **Easton**, *The Political System: An Inquiry into the State of Political Science*; **1953**: politics is the **"authoritative allocation of values"** for a society — a definition with **three** terms to be unpacked, not a slogan.
- **Values** = **"things considered valuable, whether they be spiritual or material"** — the disjunction admits status, recognition and rectitude as political stakes.
- **Allocation** = **"distribution of these things to various individuals or groups"** through **policy**; ⭐ policy consists of a **"web of decisions"**; a decision is a **"selection among alternatives"**; policy implies decision **plus implementation**.
- ⭐ **Authoritative** = those affected **"consider that they must or ought to obey it."** *Must* or *ought* — prudence **or** obligation.
- ⭐ **"Authority does not signify the use of brute force; it denotes the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance."** ⚠️ Keep *more-or-less*.
- ⭐⭐ The chapter's own voice first — **"politics as the 'authoritative allocation of values' represents a universal social phenomenon"** — and then Easton's: **"Every society provides some mechanisms, however rudimentary they may be ... An authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable."** ❗ The topic's strongest single sentence; held by no sibling edition.
- ⚠️ The collision to keep: unavoidability (p.97) against **"the end of class-conflict marks an end of politics itself"** (p.104). Staged at Lessons 8 and 10; the chapter itself never stages it.
- Progress marker: Easton **"add[s] a new dimension to our earlier formulation ... It brings in the element of authoritativeness to the process of conflict-resolution."**
- **Roberts**, *A Dictionary of Political Analysis*; **1971**: politics refers **"both to an activity and to the study of that activity"**; as activity it is the process **"by which the goals of that system are selected, ordered in terms of priority ... and implemented."**
- ❗ Roberts, taking **a liberal view**, holds that politics **"involves both cooperation and the resolution of conflict ... and if necessary, coercion"** — cooperation is on the table **before** the communitarians arrive.
- ⚠️ Roberts's hedges: politics **"usually"** involves groups, **"including sometimes groups of a specifically political type, such as political parties."** ❌ Dropping them restores Ball's problem [2].
- The demarcation: economics **"may be concerned with public or private allocation"**; politics is **"exclusively concerned with the 'public' allocation of resources in pursuance of 'public goals'."**

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the chapter insist on unpacking "authoritative allocation of values" into three defined terms instead of using the phrase as a definition? Answer using the chapter's own definitions, and name one thing each term rules out.

**Model answer:** Because each term carries a restriction that the phrase alone conceals. **Values** are **"things considered valuable, whether they be spiritual or material"** — which rules out reading politics as the distribution of material goods only, and so admits status, recognition and the chapter's own **rectitude** and **deference** as political stakes. **Allocation** is **"distribution of these things to various individuals or groups"** accomplished **"through policy which consists of a 'web of decisions'"**, where a decision is a **"selection among alternatives"** and policy implies **"arriving at a decision as well as its implementation"** — which rules out treating a single order as a policy, and therefore rules out the common error of analysing politics one decision at a time. **Authoritative** means that those affected **"consider that they must or ought to obey it"**, and the chapter adds that **"authority does not signify the use of brute force; it denotes the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance"** — which rules out identifying authority with coercion. ❗ Unpacked, the formula also supports the chapter's strongest claim, that **"an authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable"**, because what is said to be unavoidable is a **function** — deciding **"who is to get what there is of desirable things"** — not an institution.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the *must or ought* pair as a definition of legitimacy. Easton's definition is deliberately disjunctive and is satisfied by either limb, and the chapter will shortly list **"purely from fear of consequences"** among the grounds of authoritativeness. Reading *must or ought* as *ought* alone produces a consent theory the chapter does not hold, and it destroys the tension that Lesson 5 is built on.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "An authoritative allocation of some values is unavoidable." Explain what this claim asserts, and state precisely what would be required to refute it. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** **The claim is a universality thesis about a function.** Easton (*The Political System*; 1953), quoted by Gauba, holds that **"every society provides some mechanisms, however rudimentary they may be, for authoritatively resolving differences about the ends that are to be pursued, that is for deciding who is to get what there is of desirable things."** Three features fix its strength: the scope is **every society**; **"however rudimentary"** denies that simple institutions count as absence of the mechanism; and what is unavoidable is an **allocative function**, not a state or a bureaucracy. ⚠️ **Refutation therefore requires more than abolishing an institution.** It requires exhibiting a society where differences about ends do not arise, or are settled without any mechanism treated as binding — so that nothing is **"authoritative"**, a policy binding because those affected **"must or ought to obey it."** Removing the state leaves the function untouched.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 3 + 2 + 1 + 1):** 3 for reproducing the quotation accurately with **"however rudimentary they may be"** retained, since that clause is what blocks the easiest objection; 3 for identifying the object of the claim as a **function** (deciding who gets what) rather than an institution, which is the move the refutation half depends on; 2 for stating the refutation condition in terms the chapter supplies — no mechanism treated as binding, tested against the *must or ought* definition; 1 for dating and titling Easton correctly; 1 for directive fidelity, since **Explain** requires the content and the implication rather than an evaluation. ❌ Cap at 6 if the claim is rendered as "the state is necessary", which is not what is asserted.

❗ **This is a whole printed concept that appears in neither owner in any form and in no sibling edition.** The word *politicization* does not occur anywhere in the canonical Markdown for this topic; nor does any of the five characteristic sub-heads that follow it. What the section supplies is a **measure of how much politics a society has**, a **three-term test for when a conflict arises at all**, and a **conversion rule** for how a private matter becomes a public one. All three are reusable against any stem; the last is the mechanism on which the entire public/private literature turns.

*A cube that shows conflict as an intersection, a dial that shows politics as a quantity, and a conversion rule that shows a private matter crossing into the political. Three different shapes because they do three different jobs.*

### 1. Politicization — the chapter's measure of how much politics there is

**"The term 'politicization' may be applied either to issues, (e.g. politicization of caste) or to human beings. In the latter case it implies the drawing of individuals or groups into political activity, particularly giving them a role in the making of public decisions."**

❗ A whole printed concept absent from every owner and every sibling edition. Its value is that it converts *politics* from a yes/no property of a society into a **quantity** that can be high or low, and it gives four illustrations placing regimes on a scale: absolute monarchy at the low end, ancient Greek city-states high, a military dictatorship confined to the junta, and democracy as the only form where **"politicization is intended to be universal."**

⚠️ **Read "intended to be".** Even of democracy the chapter claims an intention, not an achievement — which is a hedge, and which means the sentence cannot be used to assert that any actual democracy achieves universal politicization.

❗ **R8, and it is not optional.** The Greek illustration carries a qualification in the same sentence: the city-states represented a high level of politicization **"by drawing all 'freemen' into political activity; yet it was by no means universal since it excluded the very large majority of slaves and aliens."** ⭐ **Reproduce the qualification every single time the example is used.** Dropping it produces the standard and easily-penalised romanticisation of Athenian democracy, and the qualification is more useful than the example: it shows that a high *rate* of participation among the enfranchised is compatible with the exclusion of **"the very large majority"** of the population, which is a transferable analytical point.

And the clause that completes the scale: **"when an emergency is declared, politicization is restricted to the ruling groups as long as the declaration of emergency is in operation."** ⚠️ The dial runs **both ways** — this is the chapter's own observation that a democratic polity's level of politicization can be reduced by a formal act and restored when it lapses. ❌ No constitutional or historical emergency content may be drawn from here: the chapter supplies no country, no article, no period and no instance. ➡️ Emergency provisions are `Polity\…`'s; democracy as a form of government is **Topic 22**'s; ⚠️ and the only clause this topic lends to a Topic 22-adjacent stem is **"it is only under a democracy that politicization is intended to be universal."**

⚠️ **R17, and it is short.** The parenthesis **"(e.g. politicization of caste)"** is the chapter's **entire** treatment of caste in pp.94–107. Reproduce the parenthesis; ❌ add nothing whatever. ➡️ Caste in every form belongs to `Philosophy\paper-2\socio-political\Caste-Gandhi-Ambedkar.md` and the Indian-Society caste owners.

⚠️ **R7, stated here at the point of use.** All four regime illustrations are 2009-edition assertions: no country is named for the military dictatorship, no period for the monarchy, no source for the Greek exclusion ratio, no instance for the emergency. They are reproduced as **the chapter's claims**, never as demonstrated results.

### 2. C1 — the three-condition test, stated by elimination

The first printed characteristic is **Politics Involves Conflict or Dispute Regarding Allocation of Values**, and the chapter argues for it by removing the two cases where conflict fails to arise:

**"If something is valuable but available in abundance, there will be no dispute for its allocation. If something is both valuable and rare but the people are just complacent or ascetic, there is hardly any possibility of conflict over its allocation. Conflict occurs when something is valuable, rare and desired by many, thus giving rise to a political situation."**

❗ **A three-term necessary-condition test — valuable × rare × desired-by-many — established by elimination, and absent from both owners.** It is the most *reusable* analytical device this topic has, because it can be run against any resource in any stem.

⚠️ **The second excluded case is the genuinely unusual one.** *Complacent or ascetic* says that scarcity alone does not generate politics: **wants** are a variable too. A society may hold something valuable and scarce and still have no politics about it if nobody is pressing a claim. ⭐ That is a point almost no competing answer will carry, and it is what makes the test a test of three conditions rather than of two.

The chapter then names the sources of claim: ⭐ **"It is the clash of interests or conflicting claims or expectations of rewards that give rise to politics."** ⚠️ **Three** sources, and the third — **expectations of rewards** — is psychological rather than material. That clause is directly useful wherever a stem concerns grievance without objective deprivation, or rising expectations outrunning provision.

**The two lists.** The prevalent values are **"power, enlightenment, wealth, well-being, health, skill, affection, rectitude and deference"** — nine, and ❗ the inclusion of **rectitude** and **deference** is what stops an answer reducing politics to material distribution. In concrete terms, claims are advanced for **"bus service, schools, hospitals, public parks, markets, offices, business and employment opportunities"** — eight, and municipal and Indian in flavour, hence costless to deploy in an Indian-context stem with no dating risk whatever.

### 3. C2 — the triple-public test and the conversion rule

The second characteristic is **Politics is Concerned with Public Goals and Decisions**, and it opens with a restriction: ⭐ **"All conflicts for resource-allocation do not give rise to a political situation. Politics is exclusively concerned with conflicts involving public goals and public issues requiring public decisions for their resolution."**

⚠️ Note the **triple repetition of 'public'** — goals, issues, **decisions**. That is the chapter's own emphasis and is worth reproducing as a three-part test, because the three are separable: a conflict can concern a public good without raising a public issue, and can raise a public issue without requiring a public decision.

Then the conversion rule, which is the most consequential sentence in the section. A conflict **"between worker and employer, between customer and shopkeeper, between tenant and landlord, between the passengers on the same bus or between the users of the same road or of different roads meeting at a crossroad becomes a public issue when it is concerned with broad categories of people and calls for a solution at the public level."**

And then the chapter's own instance: **"A conflict between husband and wife on some petty domestic affair does not present a political situation. But if it takes the form of a question of respective claims of men and women in the context of their mutual relationships demanding solution at the public level, it becomes a political question and a fit subject of politics."**

❗ **This is the chapter's statement of how the private becomes political, and it is absent from both owners.** ⚠️ It sits at **PDF p.98, inside this topic's span**, so it is this topic's to teach — but **it must be taught as a logical rule** about the conditions under which a matter crosses from the private to the public domain. ❌ **No substantive gender claim may be drawn from it here.** The chapter attaches none: it uses the example to illustrate a conversion, not to assert anything about men and women. ➡️ The sex/gender distinction, patriarchy and feminist theory are **Topic 06**'s, `basic\06_Feminism-Sex-and-Gender.md` (Gauba Ch.2 §XI, pp.84–93), whose released Mains edition owns all of it. ⭐ What Topic 07 contributes is the **two-part conversion condition** — *broad categories of people* **and** *a solution demanded at the public level* — which is portable to any private-to-public question whatever.

### 4. Curtis, and the coercion backstop stated as a possibility

The section closes on a fourth named authority, **Michael Curtis** (*Comparative Government and Politics*; 1978): **"The student of politics is normally concerned with inquiry into matters of public concern, with the behaviour and acts that may concern a society as a totality or which may ultimately be resolved by the exercise of legitimate coercion."**

⚠️ Named, titled and dated in neither owner. ❗ Its value is the closing clause, which is the chapter's only statement of the **coercion backstop** before the Marxist section arrives — and note that it is phrased as a **possibility**: *may ultimately be resolved*. It is not a definition of the state and not a claim that politics rests on force. ⭐ Reproducing *may ultimately* keeps the clause defensible; dropping it turns a careful definition into a crude one and hands the Marxist critique a point it did not have to earn. ⚠️ Note also *normally*, in the first clause: a second hedge in the same sentence. ➡️ Theories of the state belong to **Topics 10 and 13**; legitimacy theory to **Topic 15**.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

⚠️ Conceptual transfer only; ❗ no General Studies or Essay route exists for this subject and none is claimed.

- **GS-II.** The scarcity test is directly usable on any allocation stem — urban services, admissions, water, spectrum, seats — because it supplies a reason why a particular good became contested: it is valuable, it is rare, **and** it is desired by many, so removing any one condition would dissolve the politics. ⚠️ The second excluded case also supplies the uncomfortable half: where claims are not pressed, absence of politics is not evidence of absence of deprivation. ❌ No scheme, statute, article or figure is asserted from this topic; those belong to the `Polity\…`, `Governance\…` and `Social-Justice\…` owners.
- **GS-IV.** The conversion rule is a decision tool: a grievance that concerns one person and asks for a private settlement is handled one way; the same grievance reframed as a claim by **"broad categories of people"** demanding **"a solution at the public level"** is a different object and may not be settled privately without a defect of fairness. ⚠️ The ethical doctrine is the `Ethics\…` owners'.
- **Essay.** The politicization dial, with its emergency notch and its Greek qualification, makes an unusually sharp paragraph for any prompt about participation or the health of democratic life: participation is a **quantity**, it is reversible by formal act, and a high rate among the enfranchised is compatible with the exclusion of **"the very large majority."** ⚠️ Prompt scoping is `Essay\advanced\03_…`'s; ❌ no Essay prompt is claimed.

**Answer framework — the diagnostic paragraph.** Name the good or claim at issue → run the three conditions explicitly (*valuable, rare, desired by many*) and say which one is doing the work → if the stem concerns a private-looking matter, apply the conversion rule in its two parts (*broad categories* **and** *public-level solution*) → if the stem concerns participation, place the case on the dial and attach the relevant hedge (*intended to be universal*; *by no means universal*) → close on Curtis's possibility clause only where the stem concerns enforcement. ⚠️ Every illustration taken from this lesson must carry the note that it is a 2009-edition assertion.

Revision notes:

- **Politicization** applies **"either to issues, (e.g. politicization of caste) or to human beings"**; of persons it means **"the drawing of individuals or groups into political activity, particularly giving them a role in the making of public decisions."**
- The four regime markers: **absolute monarchy** = low (**"all public decisions ... by the monarch and his close associates"**); **ancient Greek city-states** = high, drawing in **"all 'freemen'"**; **military dictatorship** = **"confined to the military junta"**; **democracy** = the only form where politicization is **"intended to be universal."**
- ⭐❗ **The Greek qualification, which must always travel with the example**: **"yet it was by no means universal since it excluded the very large majority of slaves and aliens."**
- The emergency clause: **"when an emergency is declared, politicization is restricted to the ruling groups as long as the declaration of emergency is in operation."** ⚠️ The dial runs both ways; ❌ no constitutional content from here.
- ⭐ **C1's three-condition test**: abundance ⇒ no dispute; **"complacent or ascetic"** ⇒ hardly any possibility of conflict; **"conflict occurs when something is valuable, rare and desired by many."**
- ⭐ Three sources of claim: **"the clash of interests or conflicting claims or expectations of rewards"** — the third is psychological, not material.
- **Nine values**: power, enlightenment, wealth, well-being, health, skill, affection, **rectitude**, **deference**. **Eight concrete goods**: bus service, schools, hospitals, public parks, markets, offices, business and employment opportunities.
- ⭐ **C2's triple test**: politics is **"exclusively concerned with conflicts involving public goals and public issues requiring public decisions for their resolution."**
- ⭐ **The conversion rule**: a dispute becomes a public issue **"when it is concerned with broad categories of people and calls for a solution at the public level."** Two conditions, both required.
- The chapter's own instance of conversion: a petty domestic disagreement is not political, but **"if it takes the form of a question of respective claims of men and women ... demanding solution at the public level, it becomes a political question."** ⚠️ Teach as a **logical** rule; ➡️ gender substance to **Topic 06**.
- **Michael Curtis** (*Comparative Government and Politics*; 1978): politics concerns **"matters of public concern ... which may ultimately be resolved by the exercise of legitimate coercion."** ⚠️ Keep *normally* and *may ultimately*.
- ⚠️ **Dating discipline for this lesson**: all four regime illustrations are **2009-edition assertions** with no country, period or study attached. Reproduce as the chapter's claims only.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is "it is scarce" an incomplete explanation of why something becomes politically contested? Answer with the chapter's own test, and show what the missing condition explains that scarcity cannot.

**Model answer:** Because the chapter's test has **three** terms and scarcity is only one of them. It is stated by elimination: **"If something is valuable but available in abundance, there will be no dispute for its allocation. If something is both valuable and rare but the people are just complacent or ascetic, there is hardly any possibility of conflict over its allocation. Conflict occurs when something is valuable, rare and desired by many."** ❗ The second exclusion is the one scarcity cannot absorb. A good may be valuable **and** rare and still generate no politics, because the condition that completes the test is **desire**, and desire is a variable of the people rather than of the supply. That is why the chapter names three sources of claim rather than one — **"the clash of interests or conflicting claims or expectations of rewards"** — and the third, **expectations of rewards**, is explicitly psychological. ⚠️ The practical consequence for an answer is twofold: where a contested good is named, the test identifies which condition is doing the work; and where a scarce good generates **no** contest, the chapter supplies a reason that does not require assuming there is no deprivation — claims may simply not be pressed. ⚠️ All of this is 2009 argument with no study attached and is reproduced as the chapter's claim.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat the complacent-or-ascetic exclusion as a judgment on the people concerned, or as evidence that their situation is acceptable. The chapter states it as a **condition on the generation of conflict**, not as a normative verdict. Converting it into a claim that quiet implies contentment is an inference the text does not support and is precisely the kind of unsupported step a *critically examine* directive is designed to catch.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Explain the chapter's rule for when a private dispute becomes a political question, and state the two conditions it requires. *(Explain — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** **The rule is a conversion with two conjoint conditions.** A dispute — **"between worker and employer, between customer and shopkeeper, between tenant and landlord, between the passengers on the same bus"** — **"becomes a public issue when it is concerned with broad categories of people and calls for a solution at the public level."** Both limbs are required: breadth without a public-level remedy leaves private settlements, and a public-level remedy sought for one person is not a political claim. The chapter's own instance: a petty domestic disagreement **"does not present a political situation"**, but if it takes the form of **"a question of respective claims"** of categories **"demanding solution at the public level, it becomes a political question and a fit subject of politics."** ⚠️ The rule is **logical**; no substantive claim attaches. It restates the wider test — **"public goals and public issues requiring public decisions."**

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 3 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 1):** 3 for naming **both** conditions and showing that each alone is insufficient, which is what makes it a rule rather than a slogan; 2 for reproducing the chapter's own worked instance of conversion; 2 for the triple-public restatement (goals, issues, decisions) used as corroboration rather than padding; 2 for keeping the example **logical** and routing any substantive gender claim to its owner in the same breath; 1 for directive fidelity, since **Explain** requires the conditions and their joint necessity rather than an evaluation. ❌ Cap at 5 if only one condition is given; ❌ deduct 3 if the example is converted into a substantive claim about men and women.

CA found: Nothing is carried here. ⚠️ **Dating note, stated at the point of use:** the reserved-seat and taxation illustrations carry **no article, no statute, no rate and no year** and belong to a 2009 text; they are used **only** to show how authoritative allocation works.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

This lesson does three jobs the chapter does in sequence. It says what makes a resolution **meaningful**; it says what authority **rests on** and what it is **backed by**; and then, in a single printed sentence, it **forks its own argument** into liberal and Marxist branches — two sections before the three-views discussion begins. ❗ The fork is the thing to notice. Reproducing it makes the later section a **consequence** of an argument already under way, rather than a list of ideologies appended to a definition, and that difference of framing is visible to an examiner in the first two lines of a comparative answer.

⚠️ This lesson borders directly on **Topic 15**, and the rule here is to **route, not expand**. Topic 15, `basic\15_Power-Authority-and-Legitimacy.md` (Gauba Ch.13, pp.301–323), owns power, authority and legitimacy as doctrine, and its released Mains Lesson 2 owns the formula that converts power into authority. ❌ Weber's typology, the faces of power, the power/authority/influence distinctions and legitimacy theory are not taught here. ✅ What is taught here is **Easton's usage**, cited to pp.97 and 99, as an account of why a **decision** binds. ➡️ Theories of political obligation, consent and civil disobedience are **Topic 14**'s, whose released Lesson 1 is the grounds-of-obedience ladder and whose Lesson 5 is legality against legitimacy.

  !! THE CHAPTER'S OWN UNRESOLVED TENSION (U6) -- do not smooth it:
     p.97  'AUTHORITY DOES NOT SIGNIFY THE USE OF BRUTE FORCE'
     p.99  a policy may be authoritative 'PURELY FROM FEAR OF
            CONSEQUENCES'
     The chapter never reconciles them.  Teach the gap; it is the mark.

  THE TWO TAX ARROWS, WHICH CROSS:
      THE RICH ---- income tax + wealth tax ----> hospitals, schools,
                    public transport at cheaper rates
                    'LARGELY AVAILED OF BY THE POOR'
                    'THE RICH CANNOT REFUSE TO PAY'
      THE POOR ---- sales tax on 'EACH COMMODITY OF THEIR USE' ---->
                    police, defence, etc. 'CHIEFLY MEANT FOR THE
                    PROTECTION OF THE PROPERTY OF THE RICH'
                    'THE POOR, TOO, CANNOT REFUSE TO PAY'
      >> the illustration runs in BOTH directions.  Both owners carry
         only the first arrow.
      [ 2009 illustrations: no article, no statute, no rate, no year ]

  THEN THE FORK, PRINTED IN THE CHAPTER'S OWN VOICE (C5):
      'ON THIS POINT, THERE IS A DIVERGENCE BETWEEN LIBERAL AND MARXIST
       VIEWPOINTS.'
      LIBERAL  -> politics is 'AN INSTRUMENT OF RECONCILIATION OF THE
                  CONFLICTING INTERESTS IN SOCIETY'
      MARXIST  -> the interests of 'THE HAVES AND HAVE-NOTS (THE
                  PROPERTY-HOLDERS AND THE PROPERTYLESS)' are
                  'IRRECONCILABLE' ... 'SO LONG AS SOCIETY REMAINS
                  DIVIDED INTO CLASSES, THERE IS NO POSSIBILITY OF
                  CONFLICT-RESOLUTION' ... a classless society
                  'SYMBOLIZES NOT ONLY THE END OF THE CONFLICT BUT THE
                  END OF POLITICS ITSELF'
```

*A ladder whose rungs are grounds, standing on two columns of backing, capped by a revisability clause — and a fork at the foot of the page that the chapter makes two sections before anyone expects it.*

### 1. Bindingness, not agreement

⭐ **"The resolution of a political conflict is meaningful only when it is treated as binding by those who are affected by the decision embodying the resolution."**

⚠️ The criterion is **bindingness**, and the distinction matters. A settlement can be meaningful on this test while remaining disagreed with; what it cannot do is fail to bind. That pre-empts the common error of equating political resolution with consensus, and it is the clause that makes the later Marxist critique legible — because the Marxist objection at Lesson 8 is **not** that people disagree, but that the appearance of agreement is manufactured.

### 2. The three grounds, and the tension the chapter leaves open

**"According to David Easton, a policy may be accepted as authoritative on several grounds: 'moral, traditional or customary, or purely from fear of consequences' (*The Political System*; 1953). In other words, authority involves 'legitimacy' or 'power' or both."**

❗ Three grounds, resolving into a disjunction with an inclusive third option — *legitimacy* **or** *power* **or both**. Both owners carry neither the grounds nor the resolution.

⚠️ **And here is the chapter's first unresolved contradiction, which is an asset rather than a defect.** Two pages earlier Easton was quoted as holding that **"authority does not signify the use of brute force; it denotes the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance."** Here a policy may be accepted as authoritative **"purely from fear of consequences."** Compliance secured purely by fear is not obviously *more-or-less willing*, and the chapter **never stages the collision**. ❗ **Teach the gap.** The two sentences mark the boundary of the concept: at one edge authority shades into legitimacy, at the other into power, and Easton's own resolution — *legitimacy or power or both* — concedes as much without resolving it. ⭐ A candidate who names this tension, attributes it accurately to the chapter rather than inventing a critic, and uses it to show that *authority* is a **threshold concept** rather than a sharp one has produced the kind of reading that separates the top band. ⚠️ Marked as this session's staging; the chapter does not stage it. ➡️ The doctrinal resolution — if there is one — belongs to **Topic 15**.

### 3. The two-column backing, and the implementing machinery

**"Then there is a machinery to implement the authoritative decisions which symbolizes application of force or threat of physical force or coercion for the enforcement of a particular 'value-allocation'."** And then the sentence worth memorising: **"An authority is always backed by law, executive orders, custom and a strong public opinion in its favour on the one hand, and by police, magistrates and prisons on the other."**

❗ **Two columns of backing — normative on one side, coercive on the other — in a single printed sentence, and absent from both owners.** Its value is that it allows an answer to say that authority is **neither** pure consent **nor** pure force without having to construct the point: the chapter asserts both columns simultaneously and attaches **"always"** to the conjunction. ⚠️ Note that *strong public opinion in its favour* sits in the normative column as a **support**, not as a source of legitimacy in itself; the chapter does not develop it, and ❌ no theory of public opinion may be drawn from this clause.

### 4. The two tax illustrations, which the chapter runs in both directions

The chapter gives three examples of allocations treated as legitimate: seats in a public bus reserved **"for 'ladies only'"**; posts in bureaucracy and seats in legislatures reserved **"for the scheduled castes and scheduled tribes"** — both treated as legitimate **"due to the prevailing belief that the weaker sections are entitled to special protection"**; and then the pair of tax arrows.

**Arrow one:** **"The rich pay higher taxes, particularly income tax and wealth tax, for maintaining government hospitals, schools and public transport at cheaper rates, which are largely availed of by the poor. The rich cannot refuse to pay this tax on the plea that it is not utilized for their benefit, since it involves authoritative allocation."**

**Arrow two, which both owners drop:** **"On the other hand, the poor are made to pay large sums in the form of say, sales tax, on each commodity of their use, sharing the burden for the maintenance of police, defence, etc. which are chiefly meant for the protection of the property of the rich. The poor, too, cannot refuse to pay this tax since it involves authoritative allocation."**

❗ **The chapter runs the illustration in both directions**, and the second half is the unusual part: it concedes that authoritative allocation can also work **against** the worse-off, and it says so in the chapter's own voice rather than reporting it as a Marxist complaint. ⭐ Reproducing both arrows is what makes the illustration **analytical** rather than celebratory, and it is also the honest preparation for Lesson 8, where the Marxist account will say that this is the general case rather than a counterweight.

Then the clause that is decisive separately: ⭐ **"However, no authoritative allocation can be treated as eternal or unalterable. It holds the field until a new arrangement is established as authoritative."** ❗ That is the chapter's own answer to the obvious objection — *does authoritative allocation freeze injustice?* — and it is a strong one: authority is defined by **holding the field**, not by finality.

⚠️ **R7 and U11, stated here at the point of use.** All of these are **2009-edition illustrations** carrying **no article, no statute, no rate and no date**, and the chapter supplies no evidence for the incidence claims in either arrow. They are used **only** to show how authoritative allocation works. ❌ No reservation policy, no tax law, no article, no statute, no rate and no revenue figure may be asserted from this topic. ➡️ Reserved seats, reservation policy, Fundamental Rights, Directive Principles, Fundamental Duties and tax law belong to the `Polity\…` and `Social-Justice\…` owners; ➡️ caste substance to `Philosophy\paper-2\socio-political\Caste-Gandhi-Ambedkar.md` and the Indian-Society caste owners.

### 5. C4 — two conditions on an interest group, and what parties add

**"A political situation arises from a clash of interests of different large groups of society. These groups must be conscious of their particular interests, and be more or less organized for pursuing those interests."** And then: **"Political parties appear on the scene with a view to formulating large policy proposals incorporating the interests of their support-groups according to their ideologies."**

⚠️ Both owners say *interest groups* and drop **both conditions** and the party clause. ❗ **Consciousness plus organisation is a test, not a label**, and it explains something a label cannot: why a dispersed category — consumers, the unemployed, the users of a deteriorating service — can suffer a common disadvantage and still generate no politics about it. They are a category, not a group, because one or both conditions fails. ⭐ That is a genuinely useful analytical point and it pairs exactly with the *complacent or ascetic* exclusion of Lesson 4: C1 explains when a **conflict** fails to arise, C4 explains when a **party to it** fails to form.

And the party clause explains **aggregation** in one line: parties exist to convert many group interests into **"large policy proposals"**, ordered by ideology. ➡️ Interest groups and pressure groups as **institutions**, and party systems, belong to the `Governance\…` and `Polity\…` owners; ➡️ the concept of ideology is **Topic 02**'s. ❌ Nothing institutional is asserted here.

### 6. C5 — the fork, in the chapter's own voice

The fifth characteristic is **Politics is an Instrument of Conflict-resolution**, and it immediately divides: ⭐ **"On this point, there is a divergence between liberal and Marxist viewpoints."**

The liberal branch: politics **"is an instrument of reconciliation of the conflicting interests in society."** The Marxist branch: the interests of **"the two major social classes—the 'haves' and 'have-nots' (the property-holders and the propertyless)—are irreconcilable, and that, so long as society remains divided into classes, there is no possibility of conflict-resolution. A classless society, according to the Marxists, symbolizes not only the end of the conflict but the end of politics itself."**

❗ **Neither owner reproduces the fork or the parenthetical gloss on *haves* and *have-nots*.** Two things follow. First, the gloss itself — *property-holders and propertyless* — is worth carrying, because it states the **criterion** of class in this chapter, which is ownership, in three words. Second, and more important for structure: teaching the fork **here** makes the three-views section of Lessons 6 to 9 a **consequence** of a disagreement already on the page, rather than a list appended to a definition.

📝 **A note on the printed text.** The closing clause of this passage — *"into classes, there is no possibility of conflict-resolution ..."* — extracts **after** the body of p.100 in the raw text layer, because of a reading-order scramble in the scan. It has been restored to its own sentence, and the two pages are cited separately. The disclosure is recorded as normalisation N13 and is not repeated at the point of use.

### Deployment in GS-II, GS-IV and the Essay

⚠️ Conceptual transfer only; ❗ no General Studies or Essay route exists for this subject and none is claimed.

- **GS-II.** The two-column backing is an exact instrument for any stem about why a contested decision holds: it holds because it is backed **on both sides at once** — by law, orders, custom and opinion, and by the enforcement machinery — and the revisability clause supplies the balancing half, that no allocation is **"eternal or unalterable."** ⚠️ Articles, statutes, commissions and schemes are the `Polity\…` and `Governance\…` owners'; ❌ nothing constitutional is asserted here.
- **GS-IV.** The three grounds make a usable ethical discrimination: a rule complied with on **moral** grounds, on **traditional or customary** grounds, and **"purely from fear of consequences"** are three different states of an organisation, and only the first is stable under scrutiny. ⚠️ That is the chapter's vocabulary applied, not a doctrine of obligation; ➡️ obligation is **Topic 14**'s and the printed Ethics stems are the `Ethics\…` owners'.
- **Essay.** The crossing tax arrows are an unusually balanced paragraph for a prompt on taxation, redistribution or the social contract, because the chapter supplies the counter-current itself: the poor **"too, cannot refuse to pay"**, and the services their indirect taxes support are **"chiefly meant for the protection of the property of the rich."** ⚠️ Carried as the chapter's 2009 assertion with no data attached. ⚠️ Prompt scoping is `Essay\advanced\03_…`'s.

**Answer framework — the authority paragraph, and the fork sentence.** State the criterion (*binding, not agreed*) → give the three grounds and the resolution into *legitimacy or power or both* → give the two columns of backing in one sentence → add the revisability clause as the balancing move → if the stem is comparative, close the paragraph on the fork sentence, so that the comparison that follows is **the chapter's own division** rather than the candidate's. ⚠️ If the answer uses the tax illustrations, run **both** arrows or neither.

Revision notes:

- ⭐ Criterion: a resolution is **"meaningful only when it is treated as binding by those who are affected"** — bindingness, not consensus.
- ⭐ **Three grounds** (Easton, *The Political System*; 1953): **"moral, traditional or customary, or purely from fear of consequences"** ⇒ **"authority involves 'legitimacy' or 'power' or both."**
- ⚠️ **U6, the chapter's own unresolved tension:** *authority does not signify the use of brute force* (p.97) against *purely from fear of consequences* (p.99). ❌ Do not harmonise; name it as the chapter's gap. ➡️ Doctrine to **Topic 15**.
- ⭐ **Two-column backing**: **"law, executive orders, custom and a strong public opinion in its favour on the one hand, and by police, magistrates and prisons on the other."** Note **"always"**.
- The machinery **"symbolizes application of force or threat of physical force or coercion"** — threat counts as much as application.
- **Tax arrow one**: the rich pay income and wealth tax for services **"largely availed of by the poor"** and **"cannot refuse to pay."**
- **Tax arrow two** (both owners drop it): the poor pay sales tax **"on each commodity of their use"**, supporting police and defence **"chiefly meant for the protection of the property of the rich"**, and **"the poor, too, cannot refuse to pay."**
- ⭐ **Revisability**: **"no authoritative allocation can be treated as eternal or unalterable. It holds the field until a new arrangement is established as authoritative."**
- ⚠️ All illustrations in this lesson are **2009-edition** with **no article, statute, rate or year**; used only to show how authoritative allocation works. ➡️ Polity and Social-Justice owners.
- ⭐ **C4's two conditions**: groups **"must be conscious of their particular interests, and be more or less organized for pursuing those interests."** A dispersed category fails one or both.
- Parties **"appear on the scene with a view to formulating large policy proposals incorporating the interests of their support-groups according to their ideologies"** — aggregation in one line.
- ⭐ **The fork**: **"On this point, there is a divergence between liberal and Marxist viewpoints."** Liberal: **"an instrument of reconciliation."** Marxist: the **"'haves' and 'have-nots' (the property-holders and the propertyless)"** are **"irreconcilable"**, and a classless society **"symbolizes not only the end of the conflict but the end of politics itself."**

### Concept check

**Question:** Does the chapter hold that authority rests on consent or on force? Answer using its own sentences, and say what the answer costs it.

**Model answer:** **Neither, and the chapter says so twice over — but it leaves an unresolved gap doing it.** Easton's definition denies the force reading: **"authority does not signify the use of brute force; it denotes the capacity to secure more-or-less willing compliance."** But his grounds deny the pure-consent reading: a policy may be accepted as authoritative on grounds **"moral, traditional or customary, or purely from fear of consequences"**, from which the chapter concludes that **"authority involves 'legitimacy' or 'power' or both."** The backing sentence states both sides at once and makes the conjunction permanent: authority **"is always backed by law, executive orders, custom and a strong public opinion in its favour on the one hand, and by police, magistrates and prisons on the other."** ❗ **The cost is a contradiction the chapter does not stage.** Compliance obtained **"purely from fear of consequences"** is not obviously *more-or-less willing*, so the two sentences mark the outer edges of a concept rather than a single definition. ⚠️ The honest answer is that *authoritative* is a **threshold** term in this chapter — it names a decision that holds the field, and the chapter adds that nothing holds it permanently: **"no authoritative allocation can be treated as eternal or unalterable."** ⚠️ The doctrinal resolution is another owner's.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not resolve the tension by quietly dropping one half, in either direction. An answer that keeps only *not brute force* produces a consent theory the chapter does not hold; an answer that keeps only *fear of consequences* produces a coercion theory it explicitly denies. The mark is in naming the gap and attributing it to the chapter, not in tidying it away or inventing a critic to blame for it.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** Examine the claim that Gauba's account of authoritative allocation simply entrenches existing advantage. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (within 150 words):** **The claim has a basis and the chapter answers it twice.** Its basis: authority is **"always backed by law, executive orders, custom and a strong public opinion in its favour on the one hand, and by police, magistrates and prisons on the other"**, and a policy can be authoritative **"purely from fear of consequences."** ⚠️ **First answer — the illustration runs both ways.** The rich pay income and wealth tax for services **"largely availed of by the poor"**; equally, the poor pay sales tax supporting police and defence **"chiefly meant for the protection of the property of the rich."** The adverse direction is the chapter's own concession. **Second answer — authority is defined by holding, not finality:** **"no authoritative allocation can be treated as eternal or unalterable. It holds the field until a new arrangement is established as authoritative."** ⚠️ 2009 illustrations; the objection returns, stronger, under Marxism.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks: 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2):** 2 for stating the objection at full strength using the chapter's own coercive column and the *fear of consequences* ground; 2 for running **both** tax arrows rather than only the redistributive one; 2 for the revisability clause used as a structural reply rather than quoted as a flourish; 2 for attaching the dating limit at the point of use and routing the policy content to its owners; 2 for an **Examine** verdict that is graded rather than binary, and that flags the objection's return under the Marxist view. ❌ Cap at 6 if only one tax arrow appears; ❌ deduct 2 for asserting any article, statute or rate.

## Lesson 6 — Politics inside the social process, and the three images of society

Progress: 6 / 11 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: The system and sub-system argument with its two interaction products, the chapter's own statement of the question the three views answer, the three views introduced with their dated founders, the communitarian redefinition clause that fixes the Topic 21 boundary, the diagnosis of liberalism at p.101, and the precursor and exponent lists as printed

Lessons 1 to 5 built an account of politics as an activity. This lesson does the two things that turn that account into a chapter: it explains **why** politics can be studied as a process at all, and it states — in the chapter's own words — the **question** that the liberal, Marxist and communitarian views are answers to. ❗ Both are absent from the owners, and the second is the single most important framing device in this topic, because it is what keeps the three views inside **political theory** and out of the territory that Topics 03, 04 and 21 own.
